The question Τίς ἐστιν οὗτος; ("Who is this?", 21:10) answers 2:3, where Herod ἐταράχθη καὶ πᾶσα Ἱεροσόλυμα ("was troubled, and all Jerusalem") at the news of a King `[T]`. The crowd's answer, ὁ προφήτης Ἰησοῦς ("the prophet Jesus", 21:11), is true and too small: the reader knows he is the King of 21:5 `[I]`.
- **The last healing in the book is in the temple.** καὶ προσῆλθον αὐτῷ τυφλοὶ καὶ χωλοὶ ἐν τῷ ἱερῷ, καὶ ἐθεράπευσεν αὐτούς ("and the blind and the lame came to him in the temple, and he healed them", 21:14) `[T]`. θεραπεύω ("to heal") stands in 16 verses of Matthew, from 4:23 to 21:14, and 21:14 is the last (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. The healing ministry ends where it began to be summarised, with τυφλοί ("blind") and χωλοί ("lame") of 11:5 `[T]`. The verse is Matthew's alone (Mark 11 and Luke 19 lack it) `[T]`. See Tool 11 on 2 Sam 5:8.
- **"The wonderful things" and the children's cry.** The chief priests and scribes see τὰ θαυμάσια ἃ ἐποίησεν ("the wonderful things that he had done", 21:15) `[T]`. θαυμάσιος ("wonderful") occurs here only in the whole New Testament (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. Its root returns six verses later in the Ps 118 quotation: ἔστιν θαυμαστὴ ἐν ὀφθαλμοῖς ἡμῶν ("it is marvellous in our eyes", 21:42), the only θαυμαστός ("marvellous") in Matthew `[T]`, verified. The leaders see the θαυμάσια ("wonderful things") and ἠγανάκτησαν ("became indignant", 21:15) `[T]`. *Synthetic*: moderate.
- **The cry is a plea turned into praise.** Ὡσαννά ("hosanna", 21:9, 15) transliterates the Hebrew of Ps 118:25, הוֹשִׁיעָה נָּא ("save, please") `[T]`. Swete translates it, σῶσον δή ("save now", Ps 117:25) `[T]`. Matthew keeps the Hebrew sound, not the Greek gloss `[T]`. κράζω ("to cry out") marks the scene: ἔκραζον ("they were crying out", 21:9) and τοὺς κράζοντας ("those crying out", 21:15) `[T]`. The same verb is used of the crowd's οἱ δὲ περισσῶς ἔκραζον· Σταυρωθήτω ("but they kept crying out, 'Let him be crucified'", 27:23) and of Jesus' last cry (27:50) `[T]`, verified (κράζω in 12 verses, SBLGNT). The crowd of 21:9 is not said to be the crowd of 27:23 `[T]`, and the link is verbal only `[I]`, moderate.
- **"Have you never read?"** οὐδέποτε ἀνέγνωτε ("have you never read?", 21:16) is the first of two in this chapter (21:16, 42), and the phrase stands only there (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. Earlier the form was οὐκ ἀνέγνωτε ("have you not read?", 12:3, 5; 19:4; 22:31) `[T]`. The sharper adverb comes in the temple `[I]`, moderate.

**Synoptic check.** Mark has one colt and no formula quotation (Mark 11:1–7); Matthew adds 21:4–5 and the second animal `[T]`. Mark's crowd cries Εὐλογημένη ἡ ἐρχομένη βασιλεία τοῦ πατρὸς ἡμῶν Δαυίδ ("blessed is the coming kingdom of our father David", Mark 11:10); Matthew puts the title on Jesus, Ὡσαννὰ τῷ υἱῷ Δαυίδ ("Hosanna to the Son of David", 21:9) `[T]`. In Mark, Jesus looks round the temple, leaves, curses the fig tree and cleanses the temple the next day (Mark 11:11–17); Matthew joins entry and cleansing on one day and moves the fig tree after it `[T]`. Matthew omits Mark's πᾶσιν τοῖς ἔθνεσιν ("for all the nations", Mark 11:17; Luke 19:46 also lacks it) `[T]`. Matthew alone has the shaken city (21:10–11), the blind and the lame (21:14), the children and Ps 8 (21:15–16) `[T]`. Luke's Pharisees ask him to rebuke the disciples (Luke 19:39–40); in Matthew it is the chief priests and scribes, and the objectors are answered from Ps 8 `[T]`.

**Tool 11.**

**Isa 62:11 + Zech 9:9 → 21:5** *(high — formula quotation)*. *Source context:* Isa 62 is the herald's announcement to Zion after the exile. The LORD has proclaimed "to the end of the earth": εἴπατε τῇ θυγατρὶ Σειών ("say to the daughter of Zion", Isa 62:11 Swete), "behold, your salvation comes" (Hebrew הִנֵּה יִשְׁעֵךְ בָּא, "behold, your salvation comes"; Swete ὁ σωτήρ σοι παραγέγονεν, "your saviour has come"). The people will be called "the holy people, the redeemed of the LORD" (62:12) `[T]`. Zech 9 begins with judgement on the nations around Israel (9:1–8). Then the King comes to Zion "righteous and saved/saving, afflicted and riding on a donkey" (9:9). He cuts off the chariot and the war-horse and speaks peace to the nations, and his rule runs "from sea to sea" (9:10). The next verse is "because of the blood of your covenant, I have set your prisoners free" (9:11) `[T]`. The register is a summons to rejoice. *Book usage:* the ninth of ten narrator formulae (1:22 … 27:9) `[T]`. Isaiah is the book's spine; Zechariah is concentrated in 21–27 (overview). The nearest earlier formula, 12:18–21 (Isa 42:1–4), also presents a figure who does not use force: οὐκ ἐρίσει οὐδὲ κραυγάσει ("he will not quarrel or cry out", 12:19) `[T]`; the gentle King follows the quiet Servant `[I]`, moderate. *OT-to-OT:* Zech 9:9's rider on a donkey recalls Gen 49:10–11, Judah's ruler "binding his donkey (עִירֹה, "his colt") to the vine" `[I]`, moderate. *What it adds:* (1) the omitted words of Isa 62:11 ("your salvation comes") and of Zech 9:9 ("saving") are the meaning of the name Ἰησοῦς ("Jesus") given at 1:21, αὐτὸς γὰρ σώσει ("for he will save") `[I]`, moderate. The narrator does not need to quote the word: the King who comes is named for it. (2) Zech 9:11's "blood of your covenant" is the phrase that returns at 26:28 `[I]`, moderate (the overview has Exod 24:8 there). *Nearest text:* the Hebrew for the animals (υἱόν, "son", for בֶּן, "son of"); the Greek for πραΰς ("gentle") and for ὑποζυγίου ("beast of burden", Swete ὑποζύγιον). Both Swete and Rahlfs read the same `[T]`. Divergence class: **the NT's own Greek**, drawing on both.

**Ps 118:25–26 → 21:9** *(high — quotation in the crowd's mouth)*. *Source context:* Ps 118 is a thanksgiving liturgy at the temple gates. The king, hard pressed by "all nations" (118:10–12), has been rescued. He asks "open to me the gates of righteousness" (118:19). The stone the builders rejected has become the head of the corner (118:22). The festal cry follows, "save, please … prosper, please" (118:25), and the priests answer, "blessed is he who comes in the name of the LORD; we bless you from the house of the LORD" (118:26). Then "bind the festal sacrifice with cords to the horns of the altar" (118:27) `[T]`. *Book usage:* Ps 118 is cited three times: 21:9, 21:42 and 23:39 `[T]`. ὁ ἐρχόμενος ("the one who comes") recalls John's question, Σὺ εἶ ὁ ἐρχόμενος; ("Are you the one who is coming?", 11:3) `[T]`; phrase ὁ ἐρχόμενος ("the one who comes") at 11:3; 21:9; 23:39 only (SBLGNT), verified. *OT-to-OT:* the psalm's "stone" verse (118:22) is the one Jesus quotes later in the same chapter (21:42) `[T]`. *What it adds:* the crowd speaks the psalm's entry liturgy, and the gate leads to the temple, where the next scene happens `[I]`. The same psalm supplies the cornerstone (21:42) and the condition of seeing him again (23:39). *Nearest text:* εὐλογημένος ὁ ἐρχόμενος ἐν ὀνόματι κυρίου ("blessed is he who comes in the name of the Lord") is Swete Ps 117:26 verbatim; ὡσαννά ("hosanna") transliterates the Hebrew `[T]`.

**Isa 56:7 + Jer 7:11 → 21:13** *(high — γέγραπται, "it is written")*. *Source context:* Isa 56:1–8 opens the last part of Isaiah. It promises that the foreigner and the eunuch who keep the Sabbath and hold fast the covenant will be brought to "my holy mountain", their sacrifices accepted, "for my house shall be called a house of prayer for all peoples" (56:7); the LORD "gathers the outcasts of Israel" (56:8) `[T]`. Jer 7 is the temple sermon. Judah trusts "the temple of the LORD, the temple of the LORD" (7:4) while stealing, murdering and committing adultery, and then stands in the house, "Has this house, which is called by my name, become a cave of robbers (מְעָרַת פָּרִצִים, "a cave of violent ones") in your eyes?" (7:11; lemmas 4631, 6530 gated). The LORD points to Shiloh: what he did there, he will do to this house (7:12–14) `[T]`. The register moves from welcome (Isaiah) to sentence (Jeremiah). *Book usage:* γέγραπται ("it is written") is the seventh of nine (2:5; 4:4, 6, 7, 10; 11:10; 21:13; 26:24, 31) `[T]`. *OT-to-OT:* Jeremiah's Shiloh argument is the logic Jesus will speak in 23:38 and 24:2: the house is left, and no stone left on stone `[I]`, moderate–high. *What it adds:* the quotation's Jeremiah half carries the destruction of the house. Matthew cuts Isaiah's "for all the peoples" `[T]`. Whatever the reason `[I]`, the welcome of the excluded is enacted in the next verse instead: the blind and the lame come in (21:14) `[I]`, moderate. *Nearest text:* Swete Isa 56:7 and Jer 7:11 (σπήλαιον λῃστῶν, "cave of robbers", Rahlfs the same) `[T]`. Divergence class: translation loss — פָּרִיץ ("violent man") becomes λῃστής ("robber, bandit"); λῃστής ("robber") returns at 26:55 and at 27:38, 44, where two λῃσταί ("robbers") are crucified with him `[T]`, verified.

**Ps 8:3 → 21:16** *(high — "have you never read?")*. *Source context:* Ps 8 is a hymn to the Creator's name "in all the earth" (8:2, 10). God founds strength "from the mouth of babes and infants" against his enemies (8:3), then the psalmist asks "what is man … the son of man", whom God crowned and under whose feet he put all things (8:5–7) `[T]`. *Book usage:* Ps 8 returns at 22:44, where Matthew's ὑποκάτω τῶν ποδῶν σου ("under your feet") is Ps 8:7's wording (see unit 43). *OT-to-OT:* Ps 8:5's "son of man" given dominion stands beside Dan 7:13 in the book's Son of Man language `[I]`, uncertain for Matthew. *What it adds:* the infants' praise silences "the enemy and the avenger" (8:3); the leaders who object are cast in that role `[I]`. *Nearest text:* Swete and Rahlfs verbatim, αἶνον ("praise"). The WLC has עֹז ("strength"; lemma 5797 gated) `[T]`. Divergence class: substantive — the Greek's "praise" is the reading the argument needs; the overview already records this.

**2 Sam 5:8 → 21:14** *(moderate–high — new; not in the overview or the audit)*. *Source context:* David takes Jerusalem from the Jebusites, who had taunted that "the blind and the lame" would keep him out. David says that whoever strikes the Jebusites must reach "the lame and the blind, whom David's soul hates". Hence the saying: Τυφλοὶ καὶ χωλοὶ οὐκ εἰσελεύσονται εἰς οἶκον Κυρίου ("the blind and the lame shall not enter the house of the Lord", 2 Sam 5:8 Swete; Rahlfs the same) `[T]`. The WLC ends עִוֵּר וּפִסֵּחַ לֹא יָבוֹא אֶל־הַבָּיִת ("blind and lame shall not come into the house"; lemmas 5787, 6455, 1004 gated) `[T]`. The next verses: David dwells in the stronghold and calls it "the city of David" (5:9) `[T]`. *Evidence:* τυφλοὶ καὶ χωλοί ("blind and lame") in that order, nominative, occurs in Swete only at 2 Sam 5:8 `[T]`, verified (positive control: the search returns the verse itself). Matthew uses the same pair in the same order, with ἐν τῷ ἱερῷ ("in the temple"), in a scene where the Son of David has just entered David's city `[T]`. *Divergence class:* **substantive in the Greek** — "the house of the Lord" is the Greek's; the Hebrew has only "the house". So the temple exclusion that Matthew's scene reverses is explicit only in the Greek `[T]`/`[I]`. *What it adds:* the son of David opens the house that the founding saying of David's city closed to the blind and the lame `[I]`, moderate–high. This is Matthean material (no Synoptic parallel) and sits inside a Davidic frame two verses either side (21:9, 15) `[T]`. Not yet audited; recommend for the next audit queue.

*Internal:* **Answers §2:2–3** — the King (βασιλεύς, "king", 2:2 → 21:5) and the troubled city (2:3 → 21:10) (moderate–high; the overview's Echo Table). **Answers §5:5; §11:29** — πραΰς ("gentle") (high, verified). **Answers §11:3** — ὁ ἐρχόμενος ("the one who comes") (high, verified). **Answers §11:5** — τυφλοί ("blind") and χωλοί ("lame") (moderate; the last θεραπεύω ("to heal"), verified). **Answers §1:1; §20:30–31** — υἱὸς Δαυίδ ("Son of David") on the lips of the blind, then of the crowd and children (high). **Plants §21:42; §23:39** — Ps 118 (high, verified). **Plants §27:51; §28:4** — σείω ("to shake") (moderate, verified). **Plants §26:55; §27:38, 44** — λῃστής ("robber") (moderate, verified). **Plants §22:44** — Ps 8 (moderate). **Plants §27:11, 29, 37, 42** — "King" (high). Opening chapters checked: 1:21 (σώσει, "he will save") answers the omitted σῴζων ("saving") (moderate, `[I]`).

**Translations.** At 21:5 the **NASB95** reads "mounted on a donkey, **even** on a colt" `[T]`. The word "even" reads the Greek καί ("and") as explanatory, and so makes one animal. Matthew's καὶ ἐπὶ ("and on") and his narrative ("the donkey and the colt … he sat on them", 21:7) make two `[T]`. The ESV reads "on a donkey, on a colt", which leaves the question open. πραΰς ("gentle") is "gentle" in the NASB95 at 5:5, 11:29 and 21:5, so the chain is audible. The ESV has "meek" (5:5), "gentle" (11:29) and "humble" (21:5), so the chain is broken `[T]`. At 21:13 the NASB95's "robbers' den" and the ESV's "den of robbers" both lose that λῃστής ("robber") is the word used of the two crucified with Jesus. **Pulpit divergence:** the ESV congregation will hear "humble" at 21:5 and will not hear the beatitude's "meek" in it; say so if the 5:5 link is preached.

**Difficulty.** *Two animals.* Options: (a) Matthew misread Hebrew parallelism `[S]`; (b) Matthew narrates two animals because the tradition knew a donkey with its foal and he saw the Hebrew's two clauses fulfilled `[S]`; (c) Matthew reads the Hebrew parallelism as two because his narrative needs the mother beside the unridden colt `[S]`. The text supports only what it says: two animals, both quoted and narrated `[T]`. Not to be resolved from the pulpit.

**Pitfall.** "Palm Sunday triumph, Good Friday rejection: the fickle crowd." The text does not identify the crowd of 21:9 with the crowd of 27:20–23. In 21:10–11 it distinguishes "the crowds" (who call him a prophet) from "the whole city" (which asks who he is) `[T]`. The corrective: preach the entry as the King's claim on his city and his house, and let the question "Who is this?" (21:10) stand as the question the next chapters answer.

**Book-overview note:** *Extends.* (1) The 21:5 row says the two animals follow the Hebrew; add that πραΰς ("gentle") follows the Greek against עָנִי ("afflicted"), so the quotation is mixed, like 12:18–21. (2) Add 2 Sam 5:8 (τυφλοὶ καὶ χωλοί, "blind and lame", Swete only there; "house of the Lord" only in the Greek) → 21:14, moderate–high, Matthean. (3) The *Canonical Position* note says the phrase "all the nations" is "kept for the last line" (28:19). The case varies, but the idea is already present in 24:9 (πάντων τῶν ἐθνῶν, "all the nations"), 24:14 (πᾶσιν τοῖς ἔθνεσιν, "to all the nations") and 25:32 (πάντα τὰ ἔθνη, "all the nations"). The accusative phrase πάντα τὰ ἔθνη stands only at 25:32 and 28:19 (SBLGNT, verified). "Kept for the end of the book" is safer than "kept for the last line". (4) The 21:9 Echo Table row could add that 21:15 θαυμάσια ("wonderful things", NT hapax) and 21:42 θαυμαστή ("marvellous") share a root `[I]`, moderate.

---

## 42. Matthew 21:18–22:14 — The Fig Tree, Two Sons, the Tenants, the Wedding Feast ⭐

**Position.** *Q1:* the King has entered his city and his house (21:1–17), and the chief priests and scribes have seen what he did and become indignant (21:15) `[T]`. The question of 21:10, "Who is this?", is open. *Q2:* this unit exists here because the claim made by the entry must now be tested and judged. The leaders ask "By what authority?" (21:23); Jesus refuses to answer directly and answers instead with three parables, each ending in a verdict on those who refuse `[T]`. The withered fig tree comes first and acts out the verdict before it is spoken `[I]`. The unit cannot stand earlier: the parables need the temple confrontation as their setting, and they need the King of 21:5 to become the King of 22:2 `[I]`.

**Structure.**

| Verses | Section |
|---|---|
| 21:18–22 | The fig tree: "no fruit ever again", withered at once; faith that moves "this mountain" |
| 21:23–27 | "By what authority?" — John's baptism, "from heaven or from men?"; "We do not know" |
| 21:28–32 | Parable 1: two sons, one vineyard; tax collectors and prostitutes go first |
| 21:33–46 | Parable 2: the tenants; the stone; the kingdom taken and given |
| 22:1–14 | Parable 3: the wedding feast for the King's son; the man without a wedding garment |

The three parables are linked by repeated wording `[T]`. ἀπέστειλεν τοὺς δούλους αὐτοῦ ("he sent his slaves") stands at 21:34 and 22:3, and πάλιν ἀπέστειλεν ἄλλους δούλους ("again he sent other slaves") at 21:36 and 22:4, at those two places only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. The second and third parables are told as one sequence: servants sent, servants sent again, servants killed, the son, the destruction of the killers `[I]`, high. Each parable ends on a verdict about entry into the kingdom or loss of it (21:31, 43; 22:13–14) `[T]`.

**Text-first findings.**

- **Fruit is the keyword.** καρπός ("fruit") stands in 14 verses of Matthew (19 occurrences, SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. It begins with John: ποιήσατε οὖν καρπὸν ἄξιον τῆς μετανοίας ("produce fruit worthy of repentance", 3:8). It runs through the Sermon (7:16–20) and 12:33 `[T]`. Then it clusters here: the fig tree (21:19), the master's fruit (21:34 twice), tenants "who will give him the fruits in their seasons" (21:41), and the kingdom given ἔθνει ποιοῦντι τοὺς καρποὺς αὐτῆς ("to a nation producing its fruits", 21:43) `[T]`. 21:43 is the last καρπός ("fruit") in the book `[T]`. The fig tree with οὐδὲν … εἰ μὴ φύλλα μόνον ("nothing but leaves only", 21:19) is the parables' verdict in a single act `[I]`, high.
- **"At once."** καὶ ἐξηράνθη παραχρῆμα ἡ συκῆ ("and at once the fig tree withered", 21:19); Πῶς παραχρῆμα ἐξηράνθη ἡ συκῆ; ("How did the fig tree wither at once?", 21:20) `[T]`. παραχρῆμα ("at once") occurs only at these two verses (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. In Mark the curse and the withering are a day apart, with the temple between (Mark 11:12–14, 20–21). Matthew makes the act immediate and puts it after the temple `[T]`. The sandwich that Mark built is undone; Matthew's sequence is entry, temple, fig tree, then the authority question `[T]`.
- **John's way is the test.** The leaders' question (ἐξουσία, "authority", 21:23, 24, 27) is answered with John: τὸ βάπτισμα τὸ Ἰωάννου πόθεν ἦν; ("John's baptism — where was it from?", 21:25) `[T]`. Then the first parable is explained by John: ἦλθεν γὰρ Ἰωάννης πρὸς ὑμᾶς ἐν ὁδῷ δικαιοσύνης ("for John came to you in the way of righteousness", 21:32) `[T]`. δικαιοσύνη ("righteousness") stands in 7 verses of Matthew; this is the last (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. The first was 3:15, at John's baptism, πληρῶσαι πᾶσαν δικαιοσύνην ("to fulfil all righteousness") `[T]`. ἐξουσία ("authority") stands in 9 verses; after 21:27 it returns only at 28:18, ἐδόθη μοι πᾶσα ἐξουσία ("all authority has been given to me") `[T]`, verified. The question Jesus refuses to answer in the temple is answered on the mountain `[I]`, high.
- **The will of the Father.** τίς ἐκ τῶν δύο ἐποίησεν τὸ θέλημα τοῦ πατρός; ("which of the two did the will of the father?", 21:31) `[T]`. The phrase τὸ θέλημα τοῦ πατρός ("the will of the father") stands only at 7:21, 12:50 and 21:31 (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. In 7:21 those who "do the will of my Father" enter the kingdom, and those who say κύριε κύριε ("Lord, Lord") do not. The second son here says Ἐγώ, κύριε ("I [will], sir") and does not go (21:30) `[T]`. The parable is 7:21 in story form `[I]`, high.
- **Changing one's mind.** μεταμεληθείς ("having changed his mind", 21:29); οὐδὲ μετεμελήθητε ὕστερον ("you did not even change your minds afterwards", 21:32) `[T]`. μεταμέλομαι ("to regret, change one's mind") stands at 21:29, 21:32 and 27:3 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. At 27:3 it is Judas: μεταμεληθείς ("having regretted") he returns the silver `[T]`. The verb names the change the leaders refuse; its last use is a regret that does not lead to the Father's will `[I]`, moderate.
- **The vineyard is Isaiah's, and the son is killed outside it.** Matthew's ἐφύτευσεν ἀμπελῶνα καὶ φραγμὸν αὐτῷ περιέθηκεν καὶ ὤρυξεν ἐν αὐτῷ ληνὸν καὶ ᾠκοδόμησεν πύργον ("he planted a vineyard, put a hedge round it, dug a winepress in it and built a tower", 21:33) takes four verbs and three nouns from Isa 5:2 (see Tool 11) `[T]`. The tenants beat, kill and stone the slaves: ὃν δὲ ἐλιθοβόλησαν ("another they stoned", 21:35) `[T]`. Mark has no stoning (Mark 12:3–5) `[T]`. λιθοβολέω ("to stone") stands only at 21:35 and 23:37, ἡ … λιθοβολοῦσα τοὺς ἀπεσταλμένους ("you who stone those sent to you") (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. The parable and the lament are joined by Matthew's own verb `[I]`, moderate–high. The son is taken, thrown out and then killed (21:39); the order follows Gen 37:24 against Mark `[S: audit]`.
- **The kingdom is taken and given.** διὰ τοῦτο λέγω ὑμῖν ὅτι ἀρθήσεται ἀφ' ὑμῶν ἡ βασιλεία τοῦ θεοῦ καὶ δοθήσεται ἔθνει ποιοῦντι τοὺς καρποὺς αὐτῆς ("therefore I tell you, the kingdom of God will be taken from you and given to a nation producing its fruits", 21:43) `[T]`. The verse is Matthew's alone `[T]`. ἡ βασιλεία τοῦ θεοῦ ("the kingdom of God") stands here and at 12:28; 19:24; 21:31 (SBLGNT), against 32 uses of "kingdom of heaven" (overview) `[T]`. ἔθνος ("nation") is singular here. Of its 15 occurrences in Matthew (SBLGNT), the only other singulars are 24:7, ἔθνος ἐπὶ ἔθνος ("nation against nation"); every other use is plural, "the nations" (e.g. 24:9, 14; 25:32; 28:19) `[T]`, verified. The addressees are the chief priests and Pharisees, who know περὶ αὐτῶν λέγει ("he was speaking about them", 21:45) `[T]`. See Trap 3.
- **The wedding feast repeats the tenants, then turns.** The King's servants are seized, mistreated and killed (22:6). Then ὁ δὲ βασιλεὺς ὠργίσθη ("the King was angry", 22:7), sends his troops, ἀπώλεσεν τοὺς φονεῖς ἐκείνους καὶ τὴν πόλιν αὐτῶν ἐνέπρησεν ("destroyed those murderers and burned their city") `[T]`. Luke's banquet has no murder and no city (Luke 14:16–24) `[T]`. The new guests are gathered from the roads, πονηρούς τε καὶ ἀγαθούς ("both evil and good", 22:10) `[T]`. The pair "evil and good" is the order of 5:45, where the Father's sun rises ἐπὶ πονηροὺς καὶ ἀγαθούς ("on the evil and the good") `[T]`. The invitation is as wide as the Father's sun `[I]`, moderate.
- **The garment, the silence and the darkness.** The King sees a man οὐκ ἐνδεδυμένον ἔνδυμα γάμου ("not dressed in a wedding garment", 22:11). He calls him Ἑταῖρε ("Friend", 22:12) `[T]`. ἑταῖρος ("friend, comrade") stands at 20:13, 22:12 and 26:50 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified: the grumbling labourer, the guest without the garment, and Judas at the arrest. Each time it is spoken to someone who has misjudged the master's goodness `[I]`, moderate. ὁ δὲ ἐφιμώθη ("but he was silenced", 22:12) `[T]`. φιμόω ("to silence") recurs only at 22:34, where Jesus ἐφίμωσεν τοὺς Σαδδουκαίους ("silenced the Sadducees") (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. τὸ σκότος τὸ ἐξώτερον ("the outer darkness", 22:13) stands at 8:12, 22:13 and 25:30 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. The first was spoken of "the sons of the kingdom" (8:12) `[T]`.
- **Called and chosen.** πολλοὶ γάρ εἰσιν κλητοὶ ὀλίγοι δὲ ἐκλεκτοί ("for many are called, but few are chosen", 22:14) `[T]`. κλητός ("called") occurs only here in Matthew; ἐκλεκτός ("chosen, elect") stands at 22:14 and then three times in the next discourse (24:22, 24, 31) (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. The "elect" of chapter 24 are introduced by this saying `[I]`, moderate–high.

**Synoptic check.** Mark has the fig tree in two halves around the temple (Mark 11:12–14, 20–25); Matthew has one scene after it, with παραχρῆμα ("at once") twice `[T]`. The authority question is close to Mark 11:27–33 `[T]`. The two sons are Matthew's alone (21:28–32) `[T]`. The tenants follow Mark 12:1–12 with changes: the Isaiah wording is fuller (ληνόν ("winepress") for Mark's ὑπολήνιον ("vat under the winepress"); the order of the verbs is Isaiah's) `[T]`; stoning is added (21:35); the son is thrown out *then* killed (21:39) `[S: audit]`; the hearers pronounce their own sentence, Κακοὺς κακῶς ἀπολέσει αὐτούς ("he will bring those wretches to a wretched end", 21:41), where in Mark Jesus answers his own question (Mark 12:9) `[T]`; and 21:43 is added `[T]`. 21:44 has a parallel in Luke 20:18 but not in Mark `[T]`. The wedding feast shares its outline with Luke's great supper (Luke 14:16–24): a host, refused invitations, the roads `[T]`. Matthew's host is a King, the feast is for his son, the servants are killed, the city is burned, and the garment scene is added `[T]`.

**Tool 11.**

**Isa 5:1–7 → 21:33** *(high — verbal quotation without formula)*. *Source context:* the Song of the Vineyard. The prophet sings for his beloved, who planted a choice vine on a fertile hill, fenced it, cleared it, built a tower and hewed a winepress, and waited for grapes; it yielded wild grapes (5:1–2). The owner asks Judah to judge: "what more was there to do for my vineyard?" (5:3–4). He will remove the hedge and make it a waste (5:5–6). Then the key: "the vineyard of the LORD of hosts is the house of Israel … he looked for justice (מִשְׁפָּט), and behold, bloodshed (מִשְׂפָּח); for righteousness (צְדָקָה), and behold, a cry (צְעָקָה)" (5:7, WLC) `[T]`. Then six woes follow (5:8–23) `[T]`. *Book usage:* Isaiah is the book's spine. This is its only vineyard use, but the vineyard returns from 20:1–16 (ἀμπελών, "vineyard", stands in 10 verses, all in 20:1–21:41, SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. *OT-to-OT:* Ps 80:9–17 (the vine brought out of Egypt, its wall broken down) and Jer 2:21 use the same image of Israel `[I]`, moderate. *Evidence:* Swete Isa 5:2 φραγμὸν περιέθηκα … ᾠκοδόμησα πύργον … προλήνιον ὤρυξα ("I put a hedge round … built a tower … dug a wine-vat") `[T]`. φραγμὸν περιέθηκα ("I put a hedge round") occurs in Swete only at Isa 5:2 (and its repetition in Odes 4:2) `[T]`, verified. Rahlfs the same. The WLC has no hedge verb of that kind: וַיְעַזְּקֵהוּ ("he dug it") and יֶקֶב ("wine-vat"; lemma 3342 gated) `[T]`. *Nearest text:* the Greek, for the hedge; Matthew's ληνόν ("winepress") stands nearer the Hebrew יֶקֶב ("wine-vat") than Swete's προλήνιον ("fore-vat") does, but the word is ordinary `[T]`/`[I]`. *What it adds:* (1) Isaiah makes the hearers judge their own case (5:3); Matthew's hearers do just that (21:41) `[T]`. (2) Isaiah's song is followed by six woes (5:8–23); Matthew's parables are followed, after the controversies, by seven woes (23:13–36) `[I]`, moderate (pattern, not wording). (3) In Isaiah the vineyard fails; in Matthew the vineyard is fine and the **tenants** fail, so the judgement falls on the leaders, not on the vine `[T]`/`[I]`, high. This matters for Trap 3.

**Ps 118:22–23 → 21:42** *(high — "have you never read in the Scriptures")*. *Source context:* see unit 41 (Ps 118:25–26 → 21:9). The rejected stone is the king, saved from the nations who surrounded him; "this is the LORD's doing; it is marvellous in our eyes" (118:23) `[T]`. *Book usage:* the second of three Ps 118 uses (21:9, 42; 23:39) `[T]`. *OT-to-OT:* Isa 28:16 (the tested cornerstone in Zion) and Isa 8:14 (the stone of stumbling) `[I]`, moderate. *What it adds:* the son killed in the parable is the stone made head of the corner: rejection and vindication in one verse `[I]`, high. *Nearest text:* Swete Ps 117:22–23 and Rahlfs verbatim, including αὕτη ("this", feminine, rendering Hebrew זֹּאת, "this") `[T]`.

**Dan 2:34–35, 44–45 (with Isa 8:14–15) → [21:44]** *(moderate; textually uncertain)*. *Text:* NA28 prints 21:44 in double brackets; it is omitted by D 33 it sys and Origen, and read by א B C and most witnesses (NA28 apparatus) `[T]`. Nothing should rest on it. *Source context:* Nebuchadnezzar's dream: a stone cut without hands strikes the statue and grinds it to dust, and becomes a great mountain filling the earth (2:34–35). The God of heaven will set up a kingdom that will never be destroyed (2:44) `[T]`. *Evidence:* λικμήσει ("it will crush, winnow") occurs in Swete only at Job 27:21 and Theodotion Dan 2:44 `[T]`, verified; Rahlfs shows it in Theodotion only `[T]`. The OG of Dan 2:44 lacks it `[T]`. Isa 8:15 supplies the first half: πεσοῦνται καὶ συντριβήσονται ("they will fall and be broken") at the stone of stumbling (8:14) `[T]`. *What it adds:* Theodotion Dan 2:44 says the kingdom λαῷ ἑτέρῳ οὐχ ὑπολειφθήσεται ("will not be left to another people") `[T]`. In Matthew 21:43 the kingdom is "given to a nation" `[T]`. If the bracketed verse is Matthew's, it sits next to a Danielic text that says the reverse about "another people" `[I]`, uncertain. Flag for Logos.

**Gen 37:18–24 → 21:38–39** `[S: audit]` *(moderate–high; inherited from Mark and strengthened)*. Accepted as the audit has it. Text-first extension: Matthew also has καὶ λαβόντες ("and taking", 21:35) of the slaves, so the phrase καὶ λαβόντες ("and taking") stands at 21:35 and 21:39 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified; Mark 12:3 has καὶ λαβόντες ("and taking") of the first slave as well, so the doubling is not Matthew's invention `[T]`; and κληρονόμος ("heir") and κληρονομία ("inheritance") each occur only at 21:38 (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified.

**Jer 8:13 → 21:19** *(moderate — new)*. Move 1: five chapters after the temple sermon quoted at 21:13 (Jer 7:11), the LORD says "there are no grapes on the vine and no figs on the fig tree, and the leaf has withered" (WLC אֵין תְּאֵנִים בַּתְּאֵנָה וְהֶעָלֶה נָבֵל, "no figs on the fig tree, and the leaf withers"; lemmas 8384, 5929 gated). Swete and Rahlfs: οὐκ ἔστιν σῦκα ἐν ταῖς συκαῖς, καὶ τὰ φύλλα κατερρύηκεν ("there are no figs on the fig trees, and the leaves have fallen") `[T]`. The fig tree with leaves and no fruit, beside a vine that fails, follows the quotation of Jer 7:11 by a few lines in Jeremiah and by a few verses in Matthew `[I]`. Mic 7:1 and Hos 9:10 are weaker. Not in the overview or audit.

**Zeph 1:7–8 → 22:4, 11, 14** *(moderate — new)*. Move 1: the Day of the LORD is near, "for the LORD has prepared a sacrifice; he has consecrated his invited ones" (Swete ἡτοίμακεν Κύριος τὴν θυσίαν αὐτοῦ, ἡγίακεν τοὺς κλητοὺς αὐτοῦ, "the Lord has prepared his sacrifice; he has consecrated his invited ones") `[T]`. "On the day of the LORD's sacrifice I will punish the officials and the king's house, and all who are clothed in foreign garments" (Swete ἐπὶ πάντας τοὺς ἐνδεδυμένους ἐνδύματα ἀλλότρια, "on all who are clothed in foreign garments", 1:8) `[T]`. Matthew has ἡτοίμακα ("I have prepared", 22:4), τεθυμένα ("slaughtered", 22:4), τοὺς κεκλημένους ("those invited", 22:3, 4, 8), κλητοί ("called", 22:14) and ἐνδεδυμένον ἔνδυμα ("clothed in a garment", 22:11) `[T]`. ἐνδύματα ἀλλότρια ("foreign garments") occurs in Swete only at Zeph 1:8 `[T]`, verified. The WLC has הֵכִין … זֶבַח ("he prepared a sacrifice"), קְרֻאָיו ("his invited ones"; lemma 7121 gated) and לֹבְשִׁים מַלְבּוּשׁ נָכְרִי ("wearing foreign clothing"; lemmas 4403, 5237 gated) `[T]`. *What it adds:* a feast prepared by the LORD, invited guests, and judgement on a man wrongly dressed. The cluster is distinctive; the wording is partial. Recommend for the audit queue.

*Internal:* **Answers §3:8–10; §7:16–20; §12:33** — fruit (high, verified). **Answers §3:15** — δικαιοσύνη ("righteousness") and John (high, verified). **Answers §7:21** — the will of the Father, and "Lord" without doing (high, verified). **Answers §5:45** — πονηροὺς καὶ ἀγαθούς ("evil and good") (moderate). **Answers §8:12** — outer darkness (high, verified). **Answers §20:13** — ἑταῖρε ("friend") (moderate, verified). **Plants §23:37** — stoning (moderate–high, verified). **Plants §24:22, 24, 31** — the elect (moderate–high, verified). **Plants §26:50** — ἑταῖρε ("friend") to Judas (moderate, verified). **Plants §27:3** — μεταμεληθείς ("having regretted") (moderate, verified). **Plants §28:18** — ἐξουσία ("authority") answered (high, verified). **Plants §24:32** — φύλλα ("leaves"): φύλλον ("leaf") stands only at 21:19 and 24:32 (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified; the fig tree returns as a sign of nearness (moderate). Opening material checked: 3:8–10 (fruit; the axe at the root) is the earliest member of the chain.

**Translations.** At 21:43 ἔθνει ("to a nation") is "a people" in both the NASB95 and the ESV `[T]`. Both soften the word Matthew uses elsewhere for "the nations" (24:9, 14; 25:32; 28:19). At 22:11–12 the NASB95 has "wedding clothes" and the ESV "a wedding garment"; both are fair. At 22:12 both have "Friend", so the ἑταῖρε ("friend") chain with 20:13 and 26:50 is audible in both. **Pulpit divergence:** none bearing on a finding.

**Difficulty.** *(1) The order of the two sons (21:29–31).* NA28 prints the son who refuses and then goes first, and the answer ὁ πρῶτος ("the first"). B Θ ƒ¹³ 700 reverse the sons and answer ὁ ὕστερος ("the latter"); D and the Old Latin have the refusing son first but answer ὁ ἔσχατος ("the last"), the "wrong" son (NA28 apparatus) `[T]`. The sense of the parable does not change with the first two; the D reading would make the leaders answer perversely `[S]`. *(2) 21:43 and "nation".* Options: (a) the Church as a new people replacing Israel `[S]`; (b) a new leadership of the one people, since the tenants, not the vineyard, are replaced (21:41, 45) `[S]`; (c) the nations (Gentiles) as such `[S]`. The text says the kingdom is taken "from you" (the chief priests and Pharisees, 21:45) and given to "a nation producing its fruits" `[T]`. Present the options.

**Pitfall.** "God has finished with Israel; the Church has replaced the Jews." The parable's vineyard is not destroyed; the tenants are (21:41). The hearers are named as the chief priests and Pharisees (21:45), and the crowds are on Jesus' side (21:46) `[T]`. The corrective: preach 21:43 with 21:45, as the transfer of stewardship from those who refuse fruit to those who produce it. See Trap 3.

**Book-overview note:** *Extends.* (1) Add the verbatim parable links 21:34 = 22:3 and 21:36 = 22:4 to the Arc Map's account of "three parables of judgement". (2) Add Jer 8:13 → 21:19 (moderate) and Zeph 1:7–8 → 22:3–14 (moderate) to the Intertextual Map as unaudited candidates. (3) The Isa 5:1–2 row could note that φραγμὸν περιέθηκα ("I put a hedge round") is Greek-only wording, and that Matthew adds Isaiah's pattern of the hearers judging themselves (21:41). (4) The Dan 2 row: add that Theodotion Dan 2:44's "not left to another people" stands in tension with 21:43 `[I]`, uncertain.

---

## 43. Matthew 22:15–46 — Caesar, the Resurrection, the Great Commandment, David's Lord

**Position.** *Q1:* three parables have just pronounced judgement on the leaders (21:28–22:14), and the chief priests and Pharisees know that he spoke "about them" (21:45) `[T]`. The parables ended with a guest who was silenced: ὁ δὲ ἐφιμώθη ("but he was speechless", 22:12) `[T]`. *Q2:* this unit exists here because the leaders now answer the parables in their own way. They try to trap him in his words (22:15), and they fail one by one: Pharisees with Herodians, Sadducees, a lawyer from the Pharisees `[T]`. Then Jesus asks the last question, and no one can answer (22:46) `[T]`. The unit closes all public debate before the woes of chapter 23, which are spoken *about* the scribes and Pharisees to the crowds and disciples (23:1) `[T]`. It could not stand earlier: the questions are the leaders' reply to the authority question of 21:23–27, and the last of them turns the authority question back on them: whose son is the Christ? `[I]`, high.

**Structure.**

| Verses | Section |
|---|---|
| 22:15–22 | Pharisees' disciples with Herodians: tribute to Caesar |
| 22:23–33 | Sadducees: the seven brothers and the resurrection |
| 22:34–40 | A lawyer: the great commandment |
| 22:41–46 | Jesus asks: whose son is the Christ? |

Three questions put to Jesus, then one put by him `[T]`. Each of the first three is addressed Διδάσκαλε ("Teacher", 22:16, 24, 36) `[T]`. The first and third are explicitly tests (πειράζετε, "are you testing", 22:18; πειράζων, "testing", 22:35) `[T]`. Each scene ends with the hearers' reaction: they marvelled and went away (22:22), the crowds were astonished (22:33), the Sadducees were silenced (22:34), and no one could answer or dared ask any more (22:46) `[T]`. The frame is a plot (συμβούλιον ἔλαβον, "they plotted together", 22:15) and a silence (22:46) `[T]`.

**Text-first findings.**

- **The plot formula.** συμβούλιον ἔλαβον ὅπως αὐτὸν παγιδεύσωσιν ἐν λόγῳ ("they plotted together how they might trap him in what he said", 22:15) `[T]`. συμβούλιον ("plot, counsel") stands at 12:14; 22:15; 27:1, 7; 28:12 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. The first (12:14) seeks to destroy him; here the aim is a word that will trap him; at 27:1 it is to put him to death `[T]`. παγιδεύω ("to trap, ensnare") stands only here in Matthew (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified.
- **The tester returns, and is answered from Deuteronomy 6 again.** At 22:35 a lawyer asks πειράζων αὐτόν ("testing him") `[T]`. πειράζω ("to test, tempt") stands at 4:1, 3; 16:1; 19:3; 22:18, 35 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. At 4:3 the devil is ὁ πειράζων ("the tempter"), the same participle `[T]`. In the wilderness Jesus answered the tester three times from Deut 6–8, twice with κύριον τὸν θεόν σου ("the Lord your God", 4:7; 4:10). Here he answers with Deut 6:5, and the phrase κύριον τὸν θεόν σου ("the Lord your God") stands at 4:7, 4:10 and 22:37 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. The last test in public is met as the first test was met: with the Shema's chapter `[I]`, moderate–high. *Synthetic*: the chains are verified, the reading of them is moderate–high.
- **Truth is only on the flatterers' lips.** Διδάσκαλε, οἴδαμεν ὅτι ἀληθὴς εἶ καὶ τὴν ὁδὸν τοῦ θεοῦ ἐν ἀληθείᾳ διδάσκεις ("Teacher, we know that you are truthful and teach the way of God in truth", 22:16) `[T]`. ἀληθής ("truthful") and ἀλήθεια ("truth") each occur only in this verse in Matthew (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. Jesus names what lies behind it: γνοὺς δὲ … τὴν πονηρίαν αὐτῶν ("but perceiving their malice", 22:18), and πονηρία ("malice, wickedness") also stands only here (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. He calls them ὑποκριταί ("hypocrites", 22:18). ὑποκριτής ("hypocrite") stands in 13 verses (SBLGNT): five in the Sermon and 15:7, then 22:18, six times in chapter 23 (23:13, 15, 23, 25, 27, 29), and 24:51 `[T]`, verified. 22:18 is the hinge from the controversies into the woes `[I]`, moderate–high.
- **Image, and what is owed.** Τίνος ἡ εἰκὼν αὕτη καὶ ἡ ἐπιγραφή; ("Whose likeness and inscription is this?", 22:20) `[T]`. The answer is Ἀπόδοτε οὖν τὰ Καίσαρος Καίσαρι καὶ τὰ τοῦ θεοῦ τῷ θεῷ ("Then render to Caesar the things that are Caesar's, and to God the things that are God's", 22:21) `[T]`. Three threads meet here. (1) ἀποδίδωμι ("to give back, render") was the verb of the tenants who will "render" the fruits (ἀποδώσουσιν, "will pay", 21:41) `[T]`: the leaders have just been told what they owe God `[I]`, moderate. (2) τὰ τοῦ θεοῦ ("the things of God") stands at 16:23 and 22:21 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. At 16:23 Peter "does not set his mind on the things of God, but the things of men" `[T]`. (3) κῆνσος ("poll-tax") stands at 17:25; 22:17, 19 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. At 17:25–26 the kings of the earth take κῆνσον ("poll-tax") from strangers, and "the sons are free" `[T]`. εἰκών ("likeness, image") occurs only here (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified; whether it recalls Gen 1:26–27 is open (see Tool 11).
- **Resurrection, four times in one scene.** ἀνάστασις ("resurrection") stands only at 22:23, 28, 30, 31 in Matthew (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. The Sadducees λέγοντες μὴ εἶναι ἀνάστασιν ("who say there is no resurrection", 22:23) frame a case from Moses (22:24) `[T]`. Jesus answers with two failures: Πλανᾶσθε μὴ εἰδότες τὰς γραφὰς μηδὲ τὴν δύναμιν τοῦ θεοῦ ("You are mistaken, not understanding the Scriptures nor the power of God", 22:29) `[T]`. πλανάω ("to go astray, deceive") stands at 18:12–13; 22:29; 24:4, 5, 11, 24 (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. After 22:29 every use is the deception of the end (24:4–24) `[T]`.
- **"Spoken to you by God."** οὐκ ἀνέγνωτε τὸ ῥηθὲν ὑμῖν ὑπὸ τοῦ θεοῦ λέγοντος ("have you not read what was spoken to you by God", 22:31) `[T]`. τὸ ῥηθέν ("what was spoken") stands in 12 verses of Matthew (SBLGNT); 22:31 is the only one with ὑμῖν ("to you") and the only one with ὑπὸ τοῦ θεοῦ ("by God") `[T]`, verified. Elsewhere the formula reads ὑπὸ κυρίου ("by the Lord", 1:22; 2:15) or names a prophet `[T]`. Jesus uses the narrator's fulfilment wording, and makes the Sadducees its addressees: Exod 3:6 was spoken to them `[I]`, moderate–high.
- **The crowds' verdict repeats the Sermon's.** ἐξεπλήσσοντο ἐπὶ τῇ διδαχῇ αὐτοῦ ("they were astonished at his teaching", 22:33) `[T]`. The phrase stands at 7:28 and 22:33 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. The book's first discourse ends with the crowds astonished at his teaching, and so does its last public debate `[I]`, moderate–high.
- **Gathered together against the Christ.** Οἱ δὲ Φαρισαῖοι … συνήχθησαν ἐπὶ τὸ αὐτό ("the Pharisees … gathered themselves together", 22:34) `[T]`. Then Συνηγμένων δὲ τῶν Φαρισαίων ("now while the Pharisees were gathered together", 22:41), Jesus asks Τί ὑμῖν δοκεῖ περὶ τοῦ χριστοῦ; ("What do you think about the Christ?", 22:42) `[T]`. ἐπὶ τὸ αὐτό ("together") occurs only at 22:34 in Matthew (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. Ps 2:2 Swete reads οἱ ἄρχοντες συνήχθησαν ἐπὶ τὸ αὐτὸ κατὰ τοῦ κυρίου καὶ κατὰ τοῦ χριστοῦ αὐτοῦ ("the rulers gathered together against the Lord and against his Christ") `[T]`. See Tool 11.
- **The two commandments and the whole.** ἐν ταύταις ταῖς δυσὶν ἐντολαῖς ὅλος ὁ νόμος κρέμαται καὶ οἱ προφῆται ("On these two commandments depend the whole Law and the Prophets", 22:40) `[T]`. νόμος ("law") and προφήτης ("prophet") stand together in four verses (SBLGNT): 5:17; 7:12; 11:13; 22:40 `[T]`, verified. 5:17 announces that he came to fulfil the Law and the Prophets; 7:12 sums them in the golden rule; 22:40 hangs them on two commandments `[T]`. The second commandment, Ἀγαπήσεις τὸν πλησίον σου ("you shall love your neighbour", 22:39), stands at 5:43; 19:19; 22:39 (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified; ὡς σεαυτόν ("as yourself") stands at 19:19 and 22:39 only `[T]`, verified. The second is ὁμοία αὐτῇ ("like it", 22:39); ὅμοιος ("like") is otherwise Matthew's parable word ("the kingdom of heaven is like", 13:31–52; 20:1) `[T]`.
- **David's Lord.** Πῶς οὖν Δαυὶδ ἐν πνεύματι καλεῖ αὐτὸν κύριον; ("Then how does David in the Spirit call him 'Lord'?", 22:43) `[T]`. Δαυίδ ("David") stands in 15 verses (17 occurrences, SBLGNT), from 1:1 to 22:45; 22:45 is the last `[T]`, verified. The title "son of David" opened the book (1:1) and was cried by the blind, a crowd, a Canaanite woman and children (9:27; 12:23; 15:22; 20:30–31; 21:9, 15) `[T]`. Here Jesus does not deny it; he asks how the son can be Lord `[T]`. The Pharisees have no answer, οὐδὲ ἐτόλμησέν τις ἀπ' ἐκείνης τῆς ἡμέρας ἐπερωτῆσαι αὐτὸν οὐκέτι ("nor did anyone dare from that day on to ask him another question", 22:46) `[T]`. The answer is given at the trial, from the same psalm (26:64) `[I]`, high.

**Synoptic check.** The four scenes follow Mark 12:13–37 in order `[T]`. Matthew makes the first plot a συμβούλιον ("plot", 22:15) of the Pharisees, his own formula; Mark has "they sent some of the Pharisees and Herodians" (Mark 12:13) `[T]`. Matthew adds γνοὺς … τὴν πονηρίαν αὐτῶν ("perceiving their malice") and ὑποκριταί ("hypocrites", 22:18), where Mark has τὴν ὑπόκρισιν ("their hypocrisy", Mark 12:15) `[T]`. The Sadducees' Moses quotation uses ἐπιγαμβρεύσει ("shall marry [her] as next of kin", 22:24); Mark and Luke have ἵνα λάβῃ … τὴν γυναῖκα ("that he should take the wife", Mark 12:19; Luke 20:28) `[T]`. Matthew adds Ἐγώ εἰμι ("I am") to the bush text (22:32; Mark 12:26 Ἐγὼ ὁ θεός, "I am the God") and adds the crowds' astonishment (22:33) `[T]`. The great commandment is much changed. Mark's scribe is sympathetic, recites the Shema's first line (Mark 12:29) and is told he is "not far from the kingdom" (12:34) `[T]`. Matthew's questioner is a lawyer from the gathered Pharisees, "testing him"; the Shema's first line and the scribe's reply are absent; Mark's four faculties become three (καρδία, ψυχή, διάνοια — "heart, soul, mind"; no ἰσχύς, "strength"); and 22:40 is added `[T]`. Luke has the lawyer and the test at another place (Luke 10:25–27, ἐκπειράζων, "testing") `[T]`. Matthew moves Mark's closing line "no one dared to ask him any more" (Mark 12:34) to the end of the David question (22:46), so that it closes the whole series `[T]`.

**Tool 11.**

**Exod 3:6 → 22:32** *(high — quotation)*. *Source context:* at the bush, before the Name is given, God introduces himself: אָנֹכִי אֱלֹהֵי אָבִיךָ אֱלֹהֵי אַבְרָהָם אֱלֹהֵי יִצְחָק וֵאלֹהֵי יַעֲקֹב ("I am the God of your father, the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob", WLC) `[T]`. He has seen the affliction of his people and has come down to deliver them (3:7–8) `[T]`. At 3:15 the same triple title is called "my name for ever" `[T]`. *Text:* Swete and Rahlfs Ἐγώ εἰμι ὁ θεὸς τοῦ πατρός σου, θεὸς Ἀβραὰμ καὶ θεὸς Ἰσαὰκ καὶ θεὸς Ἰακώβ ("I am the God of your father, God of Abraham and God of Isaac and God of Jacob") `[T]`. Matthew keeps the Greek's Ἐγώ εἰμι ("I am"), which Mark lacks, and repeats ὁ θεός ("the God") before each patriarch `[T]`. *Nearest text:* the Greek, for ἐγώ εἰμι ("I am") `[T]`. *Book usage:* Abraham opened the book (1:1, 2, 17), was set aside as a ground of confidence (3:9) and returned with the nations at table (8:11) `[T]`. 22:32 is the last Ἀβραάμ ("Abraham") in Matthew (overview's list; SBLGNT) `[T]`. *OT-to-OT:* the patriarchal title runs from Gen 28:13 to 1 Kgs 18:36 ("LORD, God of Abraham, Isaac and Israel") `[I]`. *What it adds:* the God who "came down to deliver" names himself by men who had died. If he is still their God, they are alive to him `[I]`, high. The argument rests on the present tense of the Greek (εἰμι, "I am") and on the covenant name, not on a verb form in the Hebrew, which has none `[T]`.

**Deut 25:5–6, with Gen 38:8 → 22:24** *(high — Deut 25 by content; Gen 38:8 by wording)*. *Source context:* Deut 25:5–10 is the law of the brother-in-law: if a man dies without a son, his brother takes the widow and the firstborn bears the dead brother's name, "so that his name may not be blotted out of Israel" (25:6) `[T]`. Gen 38:8 is Judah to Onan: "go in to your brother's wife, perform the duty of a brother-in-law to her (וְיַבֵּם, 'and do the levirate', WLC; lemma 2992 gated), and raise up offspring for your brother" `[T]`. *Text:* Swete Gen 38:8 γάμβρευσαι αὐτήν, καὶ ἀνάστησον σπέρμα τῷ ἀδελφῷ σου ("marry her as kin, and raise up offspring for your brother"); Rahlfs the same `[T]`. Swete Deut 25:5 has neither verb; it reads λήμψεται αὐτήν ("he shall take her") `[T]`. Matthew's ἐπιγαμβρεύσει … καὶ ἀναστήσει σπέρμα τῷ ἀδελφῷ αὐτοῦ ("shall marry … and raise up offspring for his brother") stands nearest Gen 38:8 Greek `[T]`. The compound ἐπιγαμβρεύω ("to marry as kin") does not occur in Gen 38:8 in Swete or Rahlfs; in Swete it stands at Gen 34:9; 1 Sam 18:22–27; 1 Macc 10:54, 56 `[T]`, verified. *Book usage:* the overview has this row (Gen 38 → 1:3; 22:24), high. *What it adds:* the Sadducees' joke about a woman with seven husbands borrows the wording of Tamar's story. Tamar, the first woman in the genealogy (1:3), got her child by that law, and through that child the line runs to David and to Christ `[I]`, moderate. The scene thus asks about "raising up seed" (ἀναστήσει σπέρμα, "raise up offspring") in the chapter about ἀνάστασις ("resurrection") `[T]` for the shared root; `[I]` for any design, uncertain.

**Deut 6:5 → 22:37** *(high — quotation)*. *Source context:* the Shema. "Hear, O Israel: the LORD our God, the LORD is one" (6:4), then וְאָהַבְתָּ אֵת יְהוָה אֱלֹהֶיךָ בְּכָל־לְבָבְךָ וּבְכָל־נַפְשְׁךָ וּבְכָל־מְאֹדֶךָ ("you shall love the LORD your God with all your heart, with all your soul, and with all your might", WLC; lemma 3824 gated) `[T]`. The words are to be on the heart, taught to the children, bound on hand and forehead (6:6–9) `[T]`. *Text:* Swete (Vaticanus) reads ἐξ ὅλης τῆς διανοίας σου ("with all your mind") as the first member; Rahlfs reads ἐξ ὅλης τῆς καρδίας σου ("with all your heart"); both have ψυχῆς ("soul") and δυνάμεως ("strength") `[T]`. This is a real Swete/Rahlfs divergence. Matthew has καρδίᾳ ("heart"), ψυχῇ ("soul") and διανοίᾳ ("mind") — the heart of Rahlfs and the mind of Swete together, and no third Greek "strength" term `[T]`. He uses ἐν ὅλῃ ("with all", literally "in all"), a closer rendering of the Hebrew בְּכָל ("with all") than the LXX's ἐξ ὅλης ("out of all") `[T]`. *Nearest text:* none exactly; the preposition is nearer the Hebrew, the nouns are Greek `[T]`/`[I]`. NA28 apparatus: Θ 0107 ƒ¹³ add "strength" (from the parallels); c syˢ·ᶜ read ἰσχύϊ ("strength") for διανοίᾳ ("mind") `[T]`. *Book usage:* Deut 6:13 and 6:16 answered the tempter (4:7, 10); this is the third use of Deut 6 in Matthew `[T]`. *OT-to-OT:* Deut 10:12; 11:13; 30:6 repeat the "all your heart and all your soul" formula, and Deut 30:6 makes it the fruit of a circumcised heart `[T]` for the text, `[I]` for the bearing. Josh 22:5 Swete also reads διανοίας ("mind") `[T]`. *What it adds:* Matthew's answer to the tester is the command that Jesus kept in the wilderness `[I]`, moderate–high.

**Lev 19:18 → 22:39** *(high — quotation)*. *Source context:* Lev 19 is the holiness code's second table: "you shall not take vengeance or bear a grudge against the sons of your people, but you shall love your neighbour as yourself: I am the LORD" (19:18, WLC) `[T]`. Swete and Rahlfs ἀγαπήσεις τὸν πλησίον σου ὡς σεαυτόν ("you shall love your neighbour as yourself"); Matthew is verbatim `[T]`. *Book usage:* third citation (5:43; 19:19; 22:39) `[T]`, verified. At 5:43 it was cut short and joined to "hate your enemy"; at 19:19 it ended the list of commandments for the rich man; here it is the second on which all hangs `[T]`. *What it adds:* the verse is "like" the first (22:39) because both begin ἀγαπήσεις ("you shall love") `[T]`. Lev 19:34 extends the command to the stranger `[T]` for Leviticus; whether Matthew has 19:34 in view is uncertain.

**Ps 110:1 (Swete 109:1) with Ps 8:7 → 22:44** *(high for Ps 110; moderate for Ps 8)*. *Source context:* a psalm "of David". נְאֻם יְהוָה לַאדֹנִי שֵׁב לִימִינִי עַד־אָשִׁית אֹיְבֶיךָ הֲדֹם לְרַגְלֶיךָ ("The LORD says to my lord, 'Sit at my right hand, until I make your enemies a footstool for your feet'", WLC; lemma 1916 gated) `[T]`. The king at God's right hand is also "a priest for ever after the order of Melchizedek" (110:4), and he "will shatter kings on the day of his wrath" (110:5) `[T]`. *Text:* Swete and Rahlfs Εἶπεν ὁ κύριος τῷ κυρίῳ μου Κάθου ἐκ δεξιῶν μου ἕως ἂν θῶ τοὺς ἐχθρούς σου ὑποπόδιον τῶν ποδῶν σου ("The Lord said to my Lord, 'Sit at my right hand until I make your enemies a footstool for your feet'") `[T]`. Matthew (NA28) reads ὑποκάτω τῶν ποδῶν σου ("under your feet") for ὑποπόδιον ("footstool") `[T]`; the majority text (K W Δ ƒ¹ 𝔪) has ὑποπόδιον ("footstool"), which is assimilation to the psalm; NA28's text is read by א B D L Z Θ ƒ¹³ (NA28 apparatus) `[T]`. ὑποκάτω τῶν ποδῶν ("under the feet") stands in Swete at 2 Sam 22:10; Ps 8:7; Mal 4:3 (= 3:21 Hebrew) `[T]`, verified. Ps 8:7 reads πάντα ὑπέταξας ὑποκάτω τῶν ποδῶν αὐτοῦ ("you have put all things under his feet"; WLC תַּחַת־רַגְלָיו, "under his feet"; lemma 8478 gated) `[T]`. Mark 12:36 has the same ὑποκάτω ("under") `[T]`, so the conflation is Matthew's inheritance, not his innovation `[T]`. *Nearest text:* Greek for 22:44a; the conflation is the NT's own Greek `[T]`. *Book usage:* Ps 110:1 returns at 26:64, καθήμενον ἐκ δεξιῶν τῆς δυνάμεως ("sitting at the right hand of power") `[T]`. ἐκ δεξιῶν ("at the right hand") stands at 20:21, 23; 22:44; 25:33, 34; 26:64; 27:38 (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. Ps 8 was quoted at 21:16 `[T]`. *OT-to-OT:* Ps 8 and Ps 110 share the subjection of enemies under the feet; Ps 8 gives it to "the son of man" (בֶן־אָדָם, Ps 8:5) `[T]` for the Psalter. *What it adds:* David, "in the Spirit" (ἐν πνεύματι, 22:43), calls his son Lord. The son of David is also the Son of Man to whom all is subjected `[I]`, moderate. At 20:21–23 the mother asked for her sons to sit ἐκ δεξιῶν ("at the right") and the places were "for those for whom it has been prepared" `[T]`; 27:38 puts two robbers ἐκ δεξιῶν ("on the right") and left `[T]`.

**Ps 2:2 → 22:34, 41–42** *(moderate — new)*. Move 1: "the kings of the earth set themselves, and the rulers take counsel together (נוֹסְדוּ־יָחַד, WLC; lemmas 3162, 4899 gated), against the LORD and against his Anointed" (2:2) `[T]`. Swete and Rahlfs: οἱ ἄρχοντες συνήχθησαν ἐπὶ τὸ αὐτὸ κατὰ τοῦ κυρίου καὶ κατὰ τοῦ χριστοῦ αὐτοῦ ("the rulers gathered together against the Lord and against his Christ") `[T]`. Matthew's συνήχθησαν ἐπὶ τὸ αὐτό ("they gathered themselves together", 22:34) is the exact Greek phrase, and it is followed by the question περὶ τοῦ χριστοῦ ("about the Christ", 22:42) `[T]`. The Greek phrase is not rare: Swete has συνήχθησαν ἐπὶ τὸ αὐτό ("gathered together") at Judg 6:33; 2 Sam 10:15; Ps 2:2 `[T]`, verified, and Rahlfs the same three. Only Ps 2:2 joins it to "the Christ" `[T]`. Mark has neither the gathering nor the phrase (Mark 12:28) `[T]`. Ps 2:7 stands behind 3:17 and 17:5 ("my Son") `[I]` (overview). Acts 4:26 quotes Ps 2:2 of the leaders' gathering against Jesus `[T]` for Acts. Rating: moderate. Not in the overview or the audit.

**Gen 1:26–27 → 22:20** *(uncertain)*. Swete Gen 1:27 κατ' εἰκόνα θεοῦ ("in the image of God") `[T]`. The coin bears Caesar's εἰκών ("image"); "the things of God" might be the one who bears God's image `[I]`. Matthew does not draw this out, and εἰκών ("image") is the ordinary word for the head on a coin `[S]`. Uncertain.

*Internal:* **Answers §4:1–10** — πειράζω ("to test") and κύριον τὸν θεόν σου ("the Lord your God"), the third Deut 6 answer to a tester (moderate–high, verified). **Answers §7:28** — "astonished at his teaching" (moderate–high, verified). **Answers §5:17; §7:12** — the Law and the Prophets (high, verified). **Answers §5:43; §19:19** — Lev 19:18 (high, verified). **Answers §16:23** — τὰ τοῦ θεοῦ ("the things of God") (moderate, verified). **Answers §17:24–27** — κῆνσος ("poll-tax") and the sons who are free (moderate, verified). **Answers §21:41** — ἀποδίδωμι ("render, pay") the fruits (moderate). **Answers §12:14** — συμβούλιον ("plot") (high, verified). **Answers §1:1; §1:3** — David's son; Tamar and the levirate (high for David; moderate for Tamar). **Answers §22:12** — φιμόω ("to silence"): the guest is silenced, then the Sadducees (high, verified in unit 42). **Plants §24:4–24** — πλανάω ("to deceive") (moderate, verified). **Plants §23:13–29** — ὑποκριταί ("hypocrites") (high, verified). **Plants §26:64** — Ps 110:1 at the trial (high). **Plants §25:33–34** — ἐκ δεξιῶν ("at the right") of the King (moderate, verified). **Plants §28:6–7** — the resurrection the Sadducees deny is announced with ἠγέρθη ("he has risen") (moderate; different word, same reality). Opening material checked: 1:1 (David, Abraham), 1:3 (Tamar), 4:1–10 (the tester and Deut 6) all answered here.

**Translations.** At 22:20 εἰκών ("image") is "likeness" in both the NASB95 and the ESV `[T]`. Neither lets "image" be heard, so the Gen 1 question cannot arise for a congregation unless the preacher raises it. At 22:16 the NASB95 "You are truthful and teach the way of God in truth" keeps the double "truth"; the ESV "you are true and teach the way of God truthfully" keeps it in another form. At 22:44 the NASB95 has "beneath your feet" and the ESV "under your feet" `[T]`; both follow NA28, and neither has the psalm's "footstool". At 22:34 the NASB95 "gathered themselves together" and the ESV "gathered together" both lose any echo of Ps 2:2, whose ESV is "take counsel together" `[T]`. **Pulpit divergence:** none bearing on a finding.

**Difficulty.** *(1) 22:21 and the two spheres.* Options: (a) two realms, each with its due `[S]`; (b) a subordination — Caesar's coin is owed back to Caesar, but Caesar too stands under God's claim `[S]`; (c) an evasion that exposes the questioners, who carry the coin in the temple courts `[S]`. The text gives an imperative with two objects and no ranking stated `[T]`. *(2) How does Exod 3:6 prove the resurrection?* Options: (a) the covenant relation implies continuing life `[S]`; (b) the present "I am" implies the patriarchs exist now `[S]`; (c) God's faithfulness to the promise requires the patriarchs to receive it `[S]`. Matthew's added ἐγώ εἰμι ("I am") and the closing οὐκ ἔστιν ὁ θεὸς νεκρῶν ἀλλὰ ζώντων ("he is not the God of the dead but of the living", 22:32) give some weight to (a) and (b) `[I]`.

**Pitfall.** "Render to Caesar" as a charter for a division of life into sacred and secular. The question was a trap set by Pharisees with Herodians (22:15–16), the answer silenced it (22:22), and the unit's centre is the commandment to love God ἐν ὅλῃ ("with all", 22:37) `[T]`. The corrective: preach 22:21 inside the unit, where God's claim is "all", and Caesar's is a coin.

**Book-overview note:** *Extends.* (1) Add Ps 2:2 → 22:34, 41–42 (moderate, new) to the Intertextual Map: συνήχθησαν ἐπὶ τὸ αὐτό ("they gathered together") + τοῦ χριστοῦ ("the Christ"). (2) The Deut 6:5 row could note the Swete/Rahlfs divergence (διανοίας / καρδίας, "mind"/"heart") and that Matthew carries both. (3) The overview's echo tables should add 4:3 ὁ πειράζων ("the tempter") → 22:35 πειράζων ("testing") with κύριον τὸν θεόν σου ("the Lord your God") at 4:7, 10 and 22:37 only. (4) Add 7:28 → 22:33 (verbatim "astonished at his teaching"). (5) The Gen 38 row stands; add that ἐπιγαμβρεύω ("to marry as kin") is not Gen 38:8's own compound in Swete or Rahlfs (γαμβρεύω, "to marry as kin") — Matthew's verb is close, not verbatim.

---

## 44. Matthew 23:1–39 — The Woes, and the Lament over Jerusalem ⭐

**Position.** *Q1:* every party has questioned Jesus and been silenced, and no one dares ask him anything more (22:46) `[T]`. The last question was his: whose son is the Christ? (22:42) `[T]`. *Q2:* this unit exists here because the debate is over and the verdict must be spoken. Jesus now speaks *about* the scribes and Pharisees to the crowds and his disciples (23:1), then *to* them in seven woes (23:13–36), then to the city (23:37–39) `[T]`. It is his last public teaching in the book; after 23:39 he leaves the temple (24:1), and the discourse that follows is spoken to the disciples privately (24:3) `[T]`. The chapter cannot stand earlier: it answers the authority question of 21:23 and the riddle of 22:41–46 with a judgement, and its last line, "your house is left to you desolate" (23:38), is the hinge into the temple discourse that follows (24:1–2) `[I]`, high. The unit is the threshold of the fifth discourse, not part of it: the audience changes at 24:3, and the closing formula at 26:1 covers "all these words" `[T]` (overview).

**Structure.**

| Verses | Section |
|---|---|
| 23:1–12 | To the crowds and disciples: Moses' seat; "they say and do not do"; one Teacher, one Father, one Leader; the greatest a servant |
| 23:13–36 | Seven woes to "scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites", ending in the blood from Abel to Zechariah |
| 23:37–39 | Lament: "Jerusalem, Jerusalem"; the house left desolate; "until you say, Blessed is he who comes" |

Οὐαί ("woe") stands seven times in chapter 23 (23:13, 15, 16, 23, 25, 27, 29, SBLGNT; 23:14 is not in NA28 or SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. Six of the seven open with the same address, γραμματεῖς καὶ Φαρισαῖοι ὑποκριταί ("scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites", 23:13, 15, 23, 25, 27, 29); the third has ὁδηγοὶ τυφλοί ("blind guides", 23:16) instead `[T]`, verified. The woes move from the kingdom shut (23:13) to blood poured out (23:35) `[T]`. The chapter ends where the parable of the tenants ended: with those who kill and stone the ones sent (21:35; 23:37) `[T]`.

**Text-first findings.**

- **"They say and do not do."** πάντα οὖν ὅσα ἐὰν εἴπωσιν ὑμῖν ποιήσατε καὶ τηρεῖτε, κατὰ δὲ τὰ ἔργα αὐτῶν μὴ ποιεῖτε, λέγουσιν γὰρ καὶ οὐ ποιοῦσιν ("therefore all that they tell you, do and observe, but do not do according to their deeds; for they say things and do not do them", 23:3) `[T]`. The charge is not false teaching but the gap between saying and doing `[T]`. It is the Sermon's last test (7:21, 24, 26) turned on the teachers `[I]`, high (overview echo table).
- **Heavy loads and the heavier things.** δεσμεύουσιν δὲ φορτία βαρέα ("they tie up heavy burdens", 23:4) `[T]`. φορτίον ("burden") stands only at 11:30 and 23:4 in Matthew (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified: τὸ φορτίον μου ἐλαφρόν ἐστιν ("my burden is light", 11:30). βαρύς ("heavy") stands only at 23:4 and 23:23 `[T]`, verified: they load others with heavy burdens, and they neglect τὰ βαρύτερα τοῦ νόμου ("the weightier provisions of the law", 23:23) `[T]`. The heavy and the weighty are set against each other, and both against the light burden of 11:30 `[I]`, high.
- **One Teacher, one Father, one Leader.** εἷς γάρ ἐστιν ὑμῶν ὁ διδάσκαλος ("for one is your Teacher", 23:8); εἷς γάρ ἐστιν ὑμῶν ὁ πατὴρ ὁ οὐράνιος ("for one is your Father, he who is in heaven", 23:9); καθηγητὴς ὑμῶν ἐστιν εἷς ὁ χριστός ("one is your Leader, that is, Christ", 23:10) `[T]`. καθηγητής ("leader, instructor") occurs only here in the NT (twice in 23:10, SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. The last word is ὁ χριστός ("the Christ"), the subject of 22:42 `[T]`. Matthew dropped the Shema's "the Lord is one" from the great commandment (22:37; contrast Mark 12:29) `[T]`; four verses into the next speech, εἷς ("one") is said three times, of the Teacher, the Father and the Christ `[I]`, moderate. ῥαββί ("Rabbi") stands at 23:7, 8; 26:25, 49 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. After the disciples are told not to be called "Rabbi", the only one who calls Jesus "Rabbi" is Judas (26:25, 49) `[T]`.
- **The humble exalted.** ὅστις δὲ ὑψώσει ἑαυτὸν ταπεινωθήσεται, καὶ ὅστις ταπεινώσει ἑαυτὸν ὑψωθήσεται ("whoever exalts himself shall be humbled; and whoever humbles himself shall be exalted", 23:12) `[T]`. ταπεινώσει ἑαυτόν ("humbles himself") stands at 18:4 and 23:12 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. At 18:4 it is the child who is greatest in the kingdom `[T]`. ὁ δὲ μείζων ὑμῶν ἔσται ὑμῶν διάκονος ("the greatest among you shall be your servant", 23:11) repeats 20:26 `[T]`. διάκονος ("servant") stands at 20:26; 22:13; 23:11 only `[T]`, verified.
- **The woes answer the Beatitudes, and the beatitude of persecution in particular.** μακάριος ("blessed") stands nine times in 5:3–11, and Οὐαί ("woe") seven times in 23:13–29 (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. The pairing is a shape, not a one-to-one match `[I]`, moderate. One link is verbal. 5:11–12 blesses the disciples ὅταν … διώξωσιν ("when they persecute") them, "for so they persecuted the prophets who were before you" `[T]`. 23:34 has καὶ διώξετε ἀπὸ πόλεως εἰς πόλιν ("and persecute from city to city") the prophets Jesus sends `[T]`. διώκω ("to persecute") stands at 5:10, 11, 12, 44; 10:23; 23:34 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. The persecutors of the Beatitudes are named in the woes `[I]`, moderate–high.
- **Justice, mercy and faithfulness.** τὴν κρίσιν καὶ τὸ ἔλεος καὶ τὴν πίστιν ("justice and mercy and faithfulness", 23:23) `[T]`. ἔλεος ("mercy") stands at 9:13; 12:7; 23:23 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. The first two are Hos 6:6, ἔλεος θέλω καὶ οὐ θυσίαν ("I desire mercy and not sacrifice") `[T]`. The third is the Law's "weightier" matter `[T]`. κρίσις ("justice, judgement") here is the word of the Servant song (12:18, 20), where the Servant brings κρίσιν ("justice") to the nations `[T]`. πίστις ("faith, faithfulness") here is its only use in Matthew that is not trust in Jesus or in God's power (8:10; 9:2, 22, 29; 15:28; 17:20; 21:21) `[T]`, verified. Luke has τὴν κρίσιν καὶ τὴν ἀγάπην τοῦ θεοῦ ("justice and the love of God", Luke 11:42) `[T]`. See Tool 11 (Mic 6:8).
- **"Fill up the measure."** καὶ ὑμεῖς πληρώσατε τὸ μέτρον τῶν πατέρων ὑμῶν ("Fill up, then, the measure of [the guilt of] your fathers", 23:32) `[T]`. πληρόω ("to fulfil, fill") stands in 16 verses (SBLGNT, overview) `[T]`. 23:32 is its only imperative, and the only place where what is "filled" is the fathers' guilt `[T]`, verified by reading the 16. The book's fulfilment verb is turned: Scripture is fulfilled in Jesus; the fathers' bloodshed is filled up in his killers `[I]`, moderate–high. μέτρον ("measure") stands only at 7:2 and 23:32 `[T]`, verified: "with the measure you measure, it will be measured to you" (7:2).
- **"I send."** διὰ τοῦτο ἰδοὺ ἐγὼ ἀποστέλλω πρὸς ὑμᾶς προφήτας καὶ σοφοὺς καὶ γραμματεῖς ("therefore, behold, I am sending you prophets and wise men and scribes", 23:34) `[T]`. In Luke the speaker is ἡ σοφία τοῦ θεοῦ ("the Wisdom of God", Luke 11:49), in the future tense `[T]`. In Matthew Jesus is the sender `[T]`. He sends "scribes" too: γραμματεύς ("scribe") is otherwise mostly an opponent's title in Matthew; the exceptions are 8:19, a scribe who offers to follow, and 13:52, the scribe "discipled for the kingdom" `[T]`. Those sent will be killed, crucified, flogged ἐν ταῖς συναγωγαῖς ὑμῶν ("in your synagogues") and persecuted, as 10:17, 23 foretold `[T]`. μαστιγόω ("to flog") stands at 10:17; 20:19; 23:34 only, and 20:19 is Jesus' own passion `[T]`, verified. σταυρόω ("to crucify") stands in 10 verses, all of Jesus (27:38 adds the robbers crucified with him) except 23:34 `[T]`, verified.
- **The blood "poured out".** ὅπως ἔλθῃ ἐφ' ὑμᾶς πᾶν αἷμα δίκαιον ἐκχυννόμενον ἐπὶ τῆς γῆς ("so that upon you may fall the guilt of all the righteous blood shed on earth", 23:35) `[T]`. The form ἐκχυννόμενον ("being poured out, shed") stands at 23:35 and 26:28 only in Matthew (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. At 26:28 it is Jesus' blood τῆς διαθήκης τὸ περὶ πολλῶν ἐκχυννόμενον εἰς ἄφεσιν ἁμαρτιῶν ("of the covenant, which is poured out for many for forgiveness of sins") `[T]`. Luke 11:50 has τὸ ἐκκεχυμένον ("which has been shed"), a perfect `[T]`. Matthew's present participle is the one he will use at the Supper `[T]`. ἐφ' ὑμᾶς ("upon you") with αἷμα ("blood") is answered by τὸ αἷμα αὐτοῦ ἐφ' ἡμᾶς ("his blood be on us", 27:25) `[T]` (overview). The blood that comes "on" this generation is joined by Matthew's wording to the blood poured out "for many" `[I]`, moderate–high. This is the text's own counterweight to Trap 3.
- **The hen and the gathering.** Ἰερουσαλὴμ Ἰερουσαλήμ … ποσάκις ἠθέλησα ἐπισυναγαγεῖν τὰ τέκνα σου, ὃν τρόπον ὄρνις ἐπισυνάγει τὰ νοσσία αὐτῆς ὑπὸ τὰς πτέρυγας, καὶ οὐκ ἠθελήσατε; ("Jerusalem, Jerusalem … How often I wanted to gather your children together, the way a hen gathers her chicks under her wings, and you were unwilling", 23:37) `[T]`. Ἰερουσαλήμ ("Jerusalem") in this Hebrew-form spelling stands only here, twice (SBLGNT); everywhere else Matthew writes Ἱεροσόλυμα ("Jerusalem", 11 verses) `[T]`, verified. ἐπισυνάγω ("to gather together") stands at 23:37 (twice) and 24:31 only `[T]`, verified. What Jesus "wanted" and was refused, his angels will do at his coming: ἐπισυνάξουσιν τοὺς ἐκλεκτοὺς αὐτοῦ ("they will gather together his elect", 24:31) `[T]`. The will is the same and the verb is the same; the gathered are now "the elect" `[I]`, high on the wording.
- **The house left desolate, and "from now on".** ἰδοὺ ἀφίεται ὑμῖν ὁ οἶκος ὑμῶν ἔρημος ("Behold, your house is being left to you desolate!", 23:38) `[T]`. NA28 prints ἔρημος ("desolate") without brackets; 𝔓⁷⁷ᵛⁱᵈ B L ff² syˢ sa boᵖᵗ omit it (NA28 apparatus) `[T]`. Any link from ἔρημος ("desolate") to ἐρήμωσις ("desolation", 24:15) rests on a word that good witnesses lack `[T]`. οὐ μή με ἴδητε ἀπ' ἄρτι ἕως ἂν εἴπητε· Εὐλογημένος ὁ ἐρχόμενος ἐν ὀνόματι κυρίου ("you will not see me from now on until you say, 'Blessed is he who comes in the name of the Lord!'", 23:39) `[T]`. ἀπ' ἄρτι ("from now on") stands at 23:39; 26:29; 26:64 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. Luke 13:35 lacks it `[T]`. Each is a "from now on" spoken at the threshold of the end: no more seeing (23:39), no more drinking (26:29), and "from now on you will see the Son of Man" (26:64) `[I]`, high on the wording.

**Synoptic check.** Mark has a three-verse warning against the scribes' robes, greetings and seats (Mark 12:38–40) `[T]`. Matthew has those items (23:5–7) and much more `[T]`. The woes are double tradition with Luke 11:39–52, where they are spoken at a Pharisee's meal and divided between Pharisees and lawyers `[T]`. Matthew sets them in the temple, makes them seven, and gives all but one the single address "scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites" `[T]`. Differences with weight: ἐγὼ ἀποστέλλω ("I send", 23:34) for Luke's "the Wisdom of God said, I will send" (Luke 11:49) `[T]`; "mercy and faithfulness" for Luke's "the love of God" (Luke 11:42) `[T]`; πληρώσατε ("fill up", 23:32) has no Lukan parallel `[T]`; "Zechariah son of Barachiah … whom you murdered" (23:35) for Luke's "Zechariah, who perished" (Luke 11:51) `[T]`; ἐκχυννόμενον ("being poured out") for Luke's ἐκκεχυμένον ("which has been shed") `[T]`. The lament is nearly verbatim with Luke 13:34–35, which stands in another setting (on the way to Jerusalem) `[T]`. Matthew has ἔρημος ("desolate", NA28) and ἀπ' ἄρτι ("from now on"), which Luke lacks `[T]`. 23:8–12 (one Teacher, Father, Leader) and 23:15–22 (proselytes; oaths) are Matthew's alone `[T]`.

**Tool 11.**

**Gen 4:8–10 → 23:35** *(high — the name and the blood)*. *Source context:* Cain kills Abel in the field. "What have you done? The voice of your brother's blood (דְּמֵי אָחִיךָ, 'your brother's bloods') is crying to me from the ground" (4:10, WLC) `[T]`. Swete: φωνὴ αἵματος τοῦ ἀδελφοῦ σου βοᾷ πρὸς μὲ ἐκ τῆς γῆς ("the voice of your brother's blood cries to me from the earth") `[T]`. *Book usage:* first and only Abel; Gen 4:24 stood behind 18:22 `[S: audit]`. *What it adds:* Abel's blood "cries from the earth" (ἐκ τῆς γῆς); Matthew's righteous blood is "shed on the earth" (ἐπὶ τῆς γῆς) `[T]`. The first murder of a righteous brother heads the list `[I]`, high.

**2 Chr 24:20–22 + Zech 1:1 / Isa 8:2 → 23:35** `[S: audit]` *(high for 2 Chr 24, Hebrew only; low–moderate for the canon-span)*. Accepted as the audit has it. Text-first extensions: (1) WLC 2 Chr 24:22 reads "Joash the king did not remember the חֶסֶד ('kindness, loyal love') that Jehoiada his father had shown him" `[T]`; Swete renders it τοῦ ἐλέους ("the mercy") `[T]`. The murder of Zechariah is a failure of ἔλεος ("mercy"), the weightier matter neglected in 23:23 `[I]`, uncertain (the link runs through the Greek, the identification through the Hebrew). (2) 2 Chr 24:21 Swete καὶ ἐλιθοβόλησαν αὐτόν ("and they stoned him") — the verb of 21:35 and 23:37 `[T]`. The stoning of Zechariah and the city "that stones those sent to it" stand two verses apart in Matthew `[I]`, moderate–high.

**Jer 22:5 (with Jer 12:7; 7:34) → 23:38** *(moderate–high)*. *Source context:* Jeremiah at the king's house: "if you will not obey these words, I swear by myself, declares the LORD, that this house shall become a desolation" (כִּי־לְחָרְבָּה יִהְיֶה הַבַּיִת הַזֶּה, WLC; lemmas 2723, 1004 gated) `[T]`. Swete and Rahlfs: κατ' ἐμαυτοῦ ὤμοσα … ὅτι εἰς ἐρήμωσιν ἔσται ὁ οἶκος οὗτος ("I have sworn by myself … that this house shall become a desolation") `[T]`. Jer 12:7: "I have forsaken my house (עָזַבְתִּי אֶת־בֵּיתִי); I have abandoned my inheritance" — Swete Ἐγκαταλέλοιπα τὸν οἶκόν μου ("I have forsaken my house") `[T]`. *Book usage:* Jeremiah's temple sermon was quoted at 21:13 (Jer 7:11) `[T]`. Jer 7:34 ends that sermon with ἐρήμωσιν ("desolation") `[T]`. εἰς ἐρήμωσιν ("into a desolation") stands in Swete at 2 Chr 30:7; Ps 72:19; Jer 4:7; 7:34; 22:5; 32:4; 51:6, 22 `[T]`, verified. *OT-to-OT:* Jer 22:5 and Jer 12:7 both speak of "the house"; Dan 9:27 and 12:11 speak of the ἐρήμωσις ("desolation") that 24:15 will cite `[T]` for the wording. *What it adds:* Jer 22:5 is an oath: God swears by himself `[T]`. It follows a chapter in which the scribes swear by the temple and its gold (23:16–22) `[I]`, uncertain as design. *Nearest text:* none verbatim; ἔρημος ("desolate") is Matthew's adjective, not Jeremiah's noun `[T]`. The house is "your" house, not "my" house (contrast Jer 12:7; and 21:13, ὁ οἶκός μου, "my house") `[T]`. The temple that was "my house" at 21:13 is "your house" at 23:38 `[I]`, high on the wording.

**Ps 118:26 (Swete 117:26) → 23:39** *(high — verbatim)*. *Source context:* see unit 41. The festal procession reaches the house of the LORD: "Blessed is he who comes in the name of the LORD; we bless you from the house of the LORD" (WLC בָּרוּךְ הַבָּא בְּשֵׁם יְהוָה; lemma 1288 gated) `[T]`. Swete and Rahlfs εὐλογημένος ὁ ἐρχόμενος ἐν ὀνόματι Κυρίου ("blessed is he who comes in the name of the Lord") `[T]`; Matthew is verbatim `[T]`. *Book usage:* the third Ps 118 use (21:9, 42; 23:39); εὐλογημένος ὁ ἐρχόμενος ("blessed is he who comes") stands at 21:9 and 23:39 only (SBLGNT) `[T]`, verified. *What it adds:* the psalm's next words are "from the house of the LORD" (118:26b) `[T]`. Jesus quotes the first half and has just said the house is left desolate `[I]`, moderate. The cry of 21:9 is made the condition of seeing him again: a judgement with an "until" `[T]`/`[I]`, high.

**Mic 6:8 (with Hos 6:6; Zech 7:9) → 23:23** *(moderate — new)*. Move 1: "what does the LORD require of you but to do justice (מִשְׁפָּט), and to love kindness (חֶסֶד), and to walk humbly with your God?" (WLC; lemmas 4941, 2617 gated) `[T]`. Swete: ποιεῖν κρίμα καὶ ἀγαπᾶν ἔλεον ("to do justice and to love mercy") `[T]`. Zech 7:9 Swete: Κρίμα δίκαιον κρίνατε, καὶ ἔλεος καὶ οἰκτιρμὸν ποιεῖτε ("Judge true judgement, and practise mercy and compassion") `[T]`. Matthew's κρίσις ("justice") and ἔλεος ("mercy") pair, with a third term of relation to God (πίστις, "faithfulness"; Micah "walk humbly with your God"), fits Mic 6:8 best `[I]`. Micah's context is also a rejection of sacrifice in favour of justice and mercy (6:6–7) `[T]`, the point of Hos 6:6 at 9:13 and 12:7. Verbal match is partial (κρίσις ("justice") / κρίμα ("judgement"); no "faith" word in Micah) `[T]`. Moderate. Not in the overview or audit.

**Isa 31:5; Deut 32:11 (with Ps 91:4; Ruth 2:12) → 23:37** *(moderate — new as a cluster)*. Move 1: Isa 31:5 "like birds hovering, so the LORD of hosts will protect Jerusalem" (WLC כְּצִפֳּרִים עָפוֹת; lemma 6833 gated); Swete ὡς ὄρνεα πετόμενα, οὕτως ὑπερασπιεῖ Κύριος … ὑπὲρ Ἰερουσαλήμ ("like birds flying, so the Lord will shield … Jerusalem") `[T]`. It is the only one of these texts that names Jerusalem `[T]`. Deut 32:11 is the eagle over its νοσσιάν ("nest, brood") spreading τὰς πτέρυγας ("its wings") `[T]`. ὑπὸ τὰς πτέρυγας ("under the wings") stands in Swete at Ruth 2:12; 1 Kgs 8:6; Ps 90:4 (= 91:4) `[T]`, verified; in Ruth and the Psalm it is refuge under God's wings `[T]`. *What it adds:* the bird that shelters Jerusalem is the LORD in Isaiah; in 23:37 the one who "wanted to gather" is Jesus `[I]`, moderate. Matthew uses ὄρνις ("hen, bird"), Isaiah ὄρνεον ("bird") `[T]`. Moderate for the cluster; no single verbal source.

*Internal:* **Answers §11:28–30** — φορτίον ("burden") (high, verified). **Answers §5:10–12** — διώκω ("to persecute") and the prophets (moderate–high, verified). **Answers §3:7; §12:34** — γεννήματα ἐχιδνῶν ("brood of vipers") (high, verified; overview). **Answers §7:2** — μέτρον ("measure") (moderate, verified). **Answers §9:13; §12:7** — ἔλεος ("mercy") (high, verified). **Answers §15:14** — ὁδηγοὶ τυφλοί ("blind guides") (high, verified: ὁδηγός, "guide", only 15:14; 23:16, 24). **Answers §18:4; §20:26** — humbling and the servant (high, verified). **Answers §21:13** — "my house" becomes "your house" (moderate–high). **Answers §21:35** — λιθοβολέω ("to stone") (moderate–high, verified). **Answers §21:9** — Ps 118:26 (high, verified). **Answers §9:20; §14:36** — κράσπεδον ("fringe"): they enlarge theirs (23:5); the sick touched his (moderate, verified: 9:20; 14:36; 23:5 only). **Answers §10:17, 23** — flogging in synagogues, persecution city to city (high). **Plants §24:31** — ἐπισυνάγω ("to gather together") (high, verified). **Plants §26:28** — ἐκχυννόμενον ("poured out") (moderate–high, verified). **Plants §27:25** — blood "on" (high; overview). **Plants §26:25, 49** — ῥαββί ("Rabbi") only from Judas (moderate–high, verified). **Plants §26:29, 64** — ἀπ' ἄρτι ("from now on") (high on wording, verified). **Plants §25:2–8** — μωρός ("foolish"): μωροὶ καὶ τυφλοί ("fools and blind", 23:17); the five foolish virgins (25:2, 3, 8) (moderate, verified: 5:22; 7:26; 23:17; 25:2, 3, 8). **Plants §25:10** — κλείω ("to shut"): they shut the kingdom (23:13); the door is shut (25:10) (moderate, verified: 6:6; 23:13; 25:10). **Plants §27:61–28:1** — τάφος ("tomb"): whitewashed tombs and the prophets' tombs (23:27, 29), then the tomb of Jesus (27:61, 64, 66; 28:1) (moderate, verified). Opening material checked: 1:21 (his people saved from their sins) stands against 23:35 through 26:28 (overview Trap 3); 2:3–4 (all Jerusalem troubled; the chief priests and scribes) is the first appearance of Jerusalem and the scribes together (moderate).

**Translations.** At 23:5 the NASB95 has "tassels" and the ESV "fringes" `[T]`. At 9:20 and 14:36 both have "fringe" `[T]`. The ESV lets the congregation hear the link between the sick who touched his fringe and the scribes who lengthen theirs; the NASB95 does not. At 23:32 the NASB95 supplies "of the guilt" in italics; the ESV has "the measure of your fathers" `[T]`. At 23:39 the NASB95 has "from now on" and the ESV "you will not see me again" `[T]`; the ESV loses the ἀπ' ἄρτι ("from now on") chain with 26:29, 64. At 23:10 the NASB95 has "Leader" and the ESV "instructor" for καθηγητής ("leader, instructor"); both fair. At 23:35 the NASB95 adds "the guilt of" before "all the righteous blood"; the ESV has "all the righteous blood" `[T]`. **Pulpit divergence:** the ESV is better at 23:5 (fringes); the NASB95 better at 23:39 (from now on). Say which the congregation will hear.

**Difficulty.** *(1) "Do and observe all that they tell you" (23:3).* Options: (a) a real endorsement of the scribes' teaching where it is Moses' (23:2) `[S]`; (b) irony, since Jesus has rejected their traditions (15:3–9; 16:12) `[S]`; (c) a concession that sets up the charge: their teaching is not the problem, their doing is `[S]`. The text sets 23:3a against 23:4 and against 16:12, "beware of the teaching (διδαχῆς) of the Pharisees and Sadducees" `[T]`. *(2) "Zechariah son of Barachiah"* — see the audit; the crux is open (Logos). *(3) Whose "house"?* The temple (24:1), the city, or the ruling house `[S]`. 23:38 comes between "Jerusalem" (23:37) and the temple buildings (24:1) `[T]`.

**Pitfall.** The woes preached as an ethnic charge, or as a description of "first-century Judaism". The addressees are "scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites" (23:13–29), and the crowds and disciples are warned in the same breath (23:1, 8–12) `[T]`. The speaker weeps over the city and wanted to gather it (23:37) `[T]`. The blood that comes "upon" this generation is named in the wording of the blood "poured out for many" (23:35; 26:28) `[T]`. The corrective: preach the woes as Jesus aims them, at religion that says and does not do, and preach 23:37 as the heart of the chapter. See Trap 3. A second pitfall: preaching 23:8–10 as a ban on titles. The point is one Teacher, one Father, one Christ, and the greatest a servant (23:11) `[T]`.
